--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:

First, I identify the two black nodes, E and J. The goal is to disconnect these two nodes by removing the minimum number of other nodes.

1. **Paths Identification**:
   - Trace all possible paths from E to J:

     1. E -> H -> I -> J
     2. E -> H -> C -> O -> P -> J
     3. E -> F -> G -> X -> L -> M -> I -> J
     4. E -> H -> B -> N -> O -> P -> J
     5. E -> H -> B -> N -> Y -> O -> P -> J

2. **Analyze potential cuts**:
   - One potential node to consider is H since it is critical in many paths:
     - Remove H: Paths (1), (2), and (4) are cut.
     - However, removing H leaves paths E -> F -> G -> X -> L -> M -> I -> J and E -> H -> B -> N -> Y -> O -> P -> J, meaning a single removal of H is not sufficient.

   - Another potential node is I:
     - Remove I: Paths (1) and (3) are cut.
     - But paths E -> H -> C -> O -> P -> J and E -> H -> B -> N -> Y -> O -> P -> J remain, meaning removing I alone is insufficient.

   - In addition, identify another critical intermediary node:
     - Remove P: Cuts paths (2), (3), and (5).
     - But paths E -> H -> I -> J and possible extensions of indirect paths through N and O (if any nodes are left) may make it insufficient alone.

   - Realizing earlier, additional cuts will need to be determined:
     - H, I, and P collectively split all paths reliably when removed, effectively disconnection pathways by severing crucial branches at higher intersects.

   - Having established H, I, and P as key nodes to disconnect, any combinator smaller than three wouldn't suffice due to redundancy or bypass in interlaced pathways.

3. **Minimum Cut Set Verification**:
   - H,
   - I,
   - P

This setup effectively breaks all possible paths and ensures no other combination or smaller subset would achieve the isolation without further investigation.

Final Answer:
Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [H, I, P].

   - Removing an edge from this critical segment ensures that no path exists between F and D:
     - Removing (H, B). However, this alone is not enough because paths shift through other nodes.
     - Removing (I, J) cuts another significant connection between intermediate nodes.

   - By removing (I, J), any path from F through I to D is interrupted.
   - Further inspecting, I find (B, C) and (P, D) also become critical to fully disconnect:
     - Removing these ensures no remaining path connects back through other routes.

Therefore, the minimum set of edges to remove so that no path exists between F and D is:

1. **Identify paths between M and B:**
   - M->F->X->G->B
   - M->F->X->K->J->I->C->B
   - M->F->G->H->B
   - M->F->G->H->I->C->B
   - M->E->L->Y->N->B
   - M->E->L->Y->N->O->C->B
   - M->E->N->B
   - M->E->N->O->C->B

2. **Determine the minimal cut set:**

    - The paths from M to B can be classified into different groups based on their intermediate nodes. Specifically, the paths can be categorized roughly into three broad groups based on common intermediate points:
        - Through node F
        - Through node E
        - Through nodes C and N

    - To disconnect M and B, we must ensure that no path remains between them. This means we must break all three categorical groups of paths.

    - By just removing one of the common intermediate nodes in each group, such as F from the first group and E from the second group, we can significantly reduce the number of paths. However, it's clear that connections through Y, C, and N still provide pathways that must be severed simultaneously.

   - **Critical nodes analysis:**
     - Removing node Y would disconnect both the direct path passing through Y (M->E->L->Y->N->B) and the path through O (M->E->N->O->C->B), but would still leave multiple other paths intact.
     - Removing node C would break any path that relies on C, ensuring M and B are fully disconnected through C.

   - Focusing on our common intermediary nodes:
       - Removing node C blocks all paths going from N or O to B.
       - Removing node E block all intermediate paths through E.

     Thus, combining these observations, we need to remove at least two nodes to ensure full disconnection: E would sever multiple specific routes and C will cut from the major crossover points.

Therefore, by strategically removing these two nodes, E and C, we effectively cut all existing paths between M and B, ensuring there are no remaining connections.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [E, C].

   - The target nodes are G and I.

2. **Examine the Graph:**
   - Node G is connected to nodes X, H, and E.
   - Node I is connected to nodes H, P, and C.

3. **Trace All Possible Paths:**
   - Path 1: G -> H -> I
   - Path 2: G -> X -> K -> E -> B -> H -> I
   - Path 3: G -> H -> B -> C -> I
   - Path 4: G -> X -> K -> E -> M -> L -> Y -> O -> C -> I
   - Path 5: G -> X -> F -> E -> B -> H -> I (indirectly through H)
   - Path 6: G -> E -> B -> C -> I

   - To disconnect G from I, we need to break all paths between them.
   - Remove the edge (H, I) to break the direct path G -> H -> I.
   - Remove the edge (G, H) to break the path G -> H -> B -> C -> I.
   - Remove the edge (B, C) to ensure no path exists through B and C.

5. **Verify the Cut Set:**
   - Removing (H, I) breaks the direct path.
   - Removing (G, H) breaks the path through H and B.
   - Removing (B, C) breaks the path through B and C.
   - Removing these edges ensures that no other paths exist between G and I.

Therefore, the minimal cut set to disconnect G and I consists of the edges (H, I), (G, H), and (B, C).

**Final Answer:** Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(G, H), (H, I), (B, C)]

    - **Path 4**: D -> P -> O -> C -> F
    - **Path 5**: D -> J -> H -> C -> F
    - **Path 6**: D -> J -> H -> G -> K -> X -> C -> F

5. **Verify no smaller cut set**:
    - Removing only P prevents direct connectivity through P and thus blocks all paths where P is intermediate.
    - Removing only C eliminates connectivity through C, which is critical for multiple paths.
    - No other single node eliminates all paths because all other nodes involved in the paths still enable alternative routes via the remaining node.

   - All paths between E and D must pass through one of the edges (B, C), (C, O), (O, P), and (H, I).

     - (B, C): This cuts paths through B to C.
     - (H, I): This cuts paths through H to I.
     - (O, P): This cuts paths through O to P.

3. **Validate the cut:**
   - Removing the edges (B, C), (H, I), and (O, P) will break all possible paths between E and D, ensuring that no alternative paths exist.

Thus, the minimum number of edges that must be removed is 3.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(B, C), (H, I), (O, P)]

   - Removing \( (H, B) \) breaks the paths involving \( H \) that connect to \( J \) through \( B \) and other intermediates.

   After removing these edges:
   - \( L \) can no longer connect to \( J \) through any intermediate nodes.
   - All paths between \( L \) and \( J \) are broken.
